Text:"Let the Fixer Decide: Training Code Localizer from the Fixers Likelihood Khalil Hennara, Muhammad Hreden, Zeina Aldallal, Sara Chrouf, and Safwan AlModhayan Khobar, Saudi Arabia {hennara,hreden,aldallal,chrouf,safwan}@misraj.ai Abstract Code localizers are usually trained to predict the files reference patch edits, but the code fixer needs to read is not the same as the code it changes. We propose Fixer-Guided Localization (FGL): the localizer proposes several localizations, frozen fixer scores each by how likely it makes verified patch, and the localizer is trained on these scores with weighted supervised fine-tuning, without running tests and without reinforcement learning. We build the full pipeline on Gemma 4 E4B for the Kaggle Gemma-4 Developer Agent competition *, from 1,629 test-verified Kimi K3 trajectories. The FGL target helps the fixer find the bug as often as the teachers localization while using 27.5% less code. On the development set, one EM round moves the agent in the expected direction compared with matched control: more tasks reach patch (53 vs. 46), fewer tasks get stuck reading files that do not exist (43 vs. 54), and more tasks are solved (8 vs. 6). Training also changes how the agent works: trained agents explore about three times more than the base model and write far fewer wrong patches, and the single agent solves up to 11 of 78 tasks, against 8 for the base model. An analysis of 13 evaluation runs identifies what limits 4B agent under an 8-minute budget (time, repetition loops and edit quality) and gives practical lessons for training small coding agents. All data, code and adapters are publicly available. Language-model agents now fix many real GitHub issues on benchmarks such as SWE-bench [Jimenez et al., 2024; OpenAI, 2024]. Some explore and edit the repository freely with shell tools [Yang et al., 2024; Wang et al., 2025]; others first find the relevant code and then write patch [Xia et al., 2024]. When open models are trained for the s"

Text:"Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation of RL Experts: Toward Continual Post-Training Zhen Zhang*,1, Changyi Yang*,2, Xuehai He3, Chengzhi Liu1, Yutong Yin4, Ziyue Liu1, Zhuoran Yin2, Yangmin Li5, Zhihan Liu4, Jisen Li6, Mingyu Jin7, Chuhan Li1, Zhaotian Weng1, Ruoyu He8, Wenyao Gao9, Jiaxin Deng10, Chenwei Wu11, Hui Shen11, Wenhao Chai12, Chenyang Zhao13, Zhuofan Ying14,15, Kieran Liu14, Andrew Chen14, Pony Ma14, Zhaoran Wang4, Alkesh Patel16, Zhe Gan16, and Xin Eric Wang1 1University of California, Santa Barbara, 2Carnegie Mellon University, 3University of California, Santa Cruz, 4Northwestern University, 5NVIDIA, 6University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, 7Independent Researcher, 8University of California, Los Angeles, 9SemiAnalysis, 10SGLang-Omni, 11University of Michigan, 12Princeton University, 13LMSYS Org, 14Mind Lab, 15Columbia University, 16Apple RL post-training typically produces domain experts that must then be consolidated into one model, and as new experts keep arriving, this consolidation becomes continual process. Weight merging and multiteacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) must re-consolidate the whole model whenever an expert is added, and can erode capabilities outside the target domains. We propose Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation (CSPD), which keeps the pre-RL model frozen, distills each RL expert independently into soft prompt, and composes experts by concatenating their prompts without joint training. single soft prompt suffices to distill an RL expert, recovering at least 90% of the teachers gain in 22 of 23 math, code, and agent settings. Independently trained prompts compose well. In multi-expert setting, the average rises with every expert added, and the full composition matches MOPD (67.83 vs. 67.79). The composition generalizes best across six held-out benchmarks. Input-space adaptation adds little serving overhead, keeping throughput within 1.5% of the unadapted backbone and reaching 2.40 that of rank-4 LoRA. These results point toward c"

Text:"What Makes Good Data for Training Coding Agents? Systematic Study on SWE-Gym Yikai Zhang 1 Mai Xu 2 Yunbei Zhang 3 Qirui Jin 4 Kenan Li 5 Yuwen Jia 6 Jinwei Yao 1 Xiangyu Zhang 1 Jielun Tan 1 Jin Pan 1 Zijian Jin 7 Xiao Sun 1 Code ı Dataset Blog1. Introduction Reinforcement learning (RL) trains coding agents on software tasks whose hidden tests decide the reward, yet task collections check these tests only against the reference fix. We ask what makes task good training data, focusing on the correctness of its reward labels. We audit SWE-Gym with two LLM families, and suspected error counts only if execution reproduces it. We find that unsolved often means mislabelled: 74% of the tasks that 9B base model never solves have test that rejects correct fixes (a false negative), versus 20% of those it sometimes solves. We repair or remove such tasks, and the audited tasks that remain train better policies. At equal size and matched difficulty, models trained on them score 2.4 and 2.7 points higher on held-out tasks from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro than models trained on the original tasks, whereas simply dropping the never-solved tasks shows no such gain. We further find that wrong patches that pass (false positives) make up about quarter of all passing patches, and that new tests that catch quarter of them do not change held-out scores detectably. pass rate shows whether task yields reward, not whether that reward is right, so the reward labels themselves must be checked. To this end, we release SWE-Gym-Verified with its 1,087 audited tasks, every verdict with its executed evidence, our training sets, and the full audit pipeline. 1AMD 2University of Michigan 3Tulane University 4Georgia Institute of Technology 5University College London 6University of California, Los Angeles 7New York University. Correspondence to: Yikai Zhang <yikzhang@amd.com>. Preprint. November 23, 2026. 1 Reinforcement learning (RL) has become standard way to train coding agents (Luo et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2026), and task collections such as SWE-Gym, R2E-Gym, SWE-smith, and SWE-rebench supply the training tasks (Pan et al., 2025; Jain et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2025; Badertdinov et al., 2025). In collections mined from GitHub, task pairs repository and an issue with the tests of the pull request that resolved it (Jimenez et al., 2024). The agent sees the issue but not the tests, and its patch is rewarded only if the tests pass. The hidden tests are therefore the reward labels. Yet collection admits task after checking only that the reference fix passes the tests and the original code fails them. This check shows that the reward is attainable. It does not show that every correct fix obtains it, or that only correct fixes do. Tests can mislabel patch in two ways (Fig. 1a, b). false negative (FN) is correct fix that the tests reject, for example because test calls private helper that the issue never mentions. false positive (FP) is wrong patch that the tests accept, for example one that handles the reported case but breaks nearby code path that no test covers. Both are well documented in evaluation benchmarks, where even human screening misses many of them (Chowdhury et al., 2024; OpenAI, 2026b; Wang et al., 2026c). Training data receives coarser checks. RL recipes drop the tasks that the model never solves (Yang et al., 2026) or the groups of samples without reward variance (Yu et al., 2025b), without looking at the tests. pass rate tells whether task yields reward, not whether that reward is right. LLM judges label each tasks issue and tests only as whole (Oliva et al., 2025; Badertdinov et al., 2025; 2026). What is missing is an execution-backed measurement of both error types in real training set and controlled test of their effect on RL. We ask what makes task good training data for coding agent, and answer in two steps  (Fig. 2)  . First, we audit SWE-Gym (Pan et al., 2025) one fail-to-pass assertion at time. Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol judge each assertion against the issue. flagged assertion counts as an error only if the tests reject an executed alternative fix that follows the issue, and passing patch counts as false positive only if What Makes Good Data for Training Coding Agents? Systematic Study on SWE-Gym Figure 1. Hidden tests mislabel patches in two ways, and the two error types occur in different places. (a) false negative in iterative/dvc-3490: the test relies on private method and private flag that the issue never names. (b) false positive in pandasdev/pandas-50796: base-model patch passes every original test but ignores into=, which counterexample exposes. (c) Share of Orig tasks that are FN-flawed and share of passing patches on Clean tasks that are false positives, by the number of eight base-model samples that pass. For false positives, counts passes among the eight samples drawn for that measurement. an executed counterexample exposes it. Tasks with false negative are repaired by adding the missing requirement to the issue, or removed. The 1,087 remaining tasks form SWE-Gym-Verified. Second, we train 9B base model with GRPO on three sets of 512 tasks with matched difficulty: original tasks (Orig), audited tasks (Clean), and audited tasks with added tests against false positives (Clean+FP). fourth set, Orig+Filter, applies pass-rate filter to the original tasks, dropping the never-solved ones. We evaluate every run on held-out tasks from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro (Deng et al., 2026). We organize the study around four research questions: RQ1: Where do label errors occur? Unsolved often means mislabelled (5.2). In Orig, 74% of the neversolved tasks have test that rejects correct fixes, versus 20% of the sometimes-solved ones. False positives make up about quarter of all passing patches and are common at every difficulty level (Fig. 1c). RQ2: Which label errors reach the training signal? Pass-rate filters keep the label errors that train the policy (5.3). GRPO learns from task only if its samples rewards differ. Never-solved flawed tasks almost never carry gradient, so dropping them changes little. Sometimes-solved flawed tasks contribute fifth of the gradient-carrying groups, with or without the filter. RQ3: Do audited tasks train better policies? Yes, and pass-rate filter does not substitute for the audit (5.4). At equal size and matched difficulty, Clean scores 2.4 points above Orig on SWE-bench Verified and 2.7 on SWE-bench Pro over three seeds. Averaged over both "

Text:"Shardul Bansal 1 Seth Schilbe 1 Jarrod Barnes 1 Abstract Language model agents are increasingly asked to run ongoing multi-day operations rather than finish isolated single-turn tasks. In daily operations, todays choices reshape later observations and cash constraints because empty shelves hide lost walk-in demand, short-shipped customers quietly shrink future orders weeks later, and emergency buying drains working capital. We introduce SMB Bench, calibrated 90-day wholesale simulator with 18 causally paired tasks and an 859-run frontier trace dataset, to ask what experience and learning signal make an agent better at noticing, attributing, and acting on the delayed consequences of its own decisions in unseen businesses. Across 16 frontier models, only 3 outperform fixed buying rule; models often record relevant diagnosis in their notes without changing later purchases in time, showing that diagnosing problem, changing behavior, and improving business are separate achievements. To study how experience improves those failures in an open model (Qwen3.8-27B), we used trainingbusiness records to identify operational rules and archived frontier quarters as starting states to continue from rather than transcripts to copy. On an unseen test business under identical evaluation instructions, the trained policy followed customerprotection commitments more consistently and reduced severe-loss quarters, while the additional profit benefit remained uncertain. Carrying those commitments through the quarter exposed the cost of fulfilling them, as early-quarter stocking habits left the buyer relying on emergency supply and deteriorating supplier. The results separate recognizing delayed effect, acting on it consistently, and coordinating surrounding decisions so that the response improves profit. Text:"NoHairCache: Calibrate-Once KV Cache Eviction with One Power-Law ExponentAbstract The decay exponent α in KV-cache eviction is an intrinsic property of the model: calibrated once on single corpus, it transfers directly across domains. Attention-based accumulation (H2O) and recency-based sliding windows (StreamingLLM) are two dominant eviction frameworks, often treated as unrelated, but we show that they are the two endpoints of single power-law family: by scoring each token as si = Ai (t i)α, where Ai is cumulative attention and α controls the age penalty, H2O and StreamingLLM correspond to α = 0 and α , respectively (with StreamingLLM additionally hard-coding an attention-sink patch). The no-hair mechanism of Xiang [2026] bounds the admissible range of α, and the measured exponents agree: calibrating once on WikiText yields [0.556, 0.822] for representative model (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct). The per-model ranges are consistent across five models (410M1.5B) and independently match the optimal regions from perplexity sweeps. NoHairCache achieves the lowest perplexity across three datasets (WikiText, PG19, and arXiv) without per-dataset tuning, outperforming both H2O and StreamingLLM (p < 0.05 on all three datasets, with 0.060.10 PPL gains over StreamingLLM at budget 512). On downstream four-way multiple-choice QA, it matches or outperforms StreamingLLM across all nine (task, budget) cells, retaining 98.2% of full-cache accuracy on NarrativeQA at 25% retention versus 91.1% for StreamingLLM; per-cell McNemar tests are not significant, so QA is corroborating only. We release our calibration script and drop-in scorer upon publication.Long-context inference is often bottlenecked at generation time: every decoded token has to attend to the entire KV cache, causing memory usage to grow linearly and, as result, total attention compute to scale quadratically with sequence length. Caching every past key-value pair is therefore untenable, making token eviction unavoidable. The question now is: by what criterion should tokens be evicted? Existing eviction policies rely on diverse scoring heuristics: accumulated attention (H2O) [Zhang et al., 2023], recency windows (StreamingLLM) [Xiao et al., 2024], observation-window pooling (SnapKV) [Li et al., 2024b], query-aware relevance (Quest) [Ge et al., 2024], or even random selection. Two paradigms dominate in practice: accumulation-based methods (e.g., H2O) and recency-based methods (e.g., StreamingLLM) [Zhang et al., 2023, Xiao et al., 2024]. Although power-law decay of token influence has been observed empirically [Lin and Tegmark, 2017, Xiang, 2026], these two paradigms remain disconnected heuristics without unified parameterization. In this work, we show that they are in fact opposite endpoints of single power-law family. By scoring each cached token at decoding step as si = Ai (t i)α, (1) where Ai denotes cumulative attention and α 0 governs the age penalty, we are able to unify both strategies under single framework. The H2O and StreamingLLM scoring rules then correspond to 1 the endpoints α = 0 and α , respectively (StreamingLLM additionally hard-codes an attention sink). Consequently, designing an eviction policy reduces from choosing between competing heuristics to identifying the decay exponent α of this family, which we calibrate per layer. The exponent is an intrinsic property of the model architecture rather than the input data distribution. Measuring α for each model once on single calibration corpus (e.g., WikiText-103) yields consistent means and bulk ranges across models (such as Qwen2.5-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B), although the detailed layer-wise profiles are only partly preserved; the measured values align closely with optimal perplexity sweeps, and each models frozen α transfers unchanged to distinct domains like PG19 and arXiv. To ground α theoretically, we draw on the empirical no-hair mechanism established in our prior work [Xiang, 2026], which shows that prompts hidden-state influence on position decays as tα, with α bounded layer-wise within [0.2, 0.7] by attention-dilution geometry. The theorem constrains the exponent within family but does not select the family itself. Building on this insight, we introduce NoHairCache, lightweight, drop-in scorer that replaces the scoring function of top-k eviction policies with our power-law formulation based on offlinecalibrated, per-layer α values, incurring only single scalar multiplication per eviction step over H2O. In summary, we make the main following contributions: Intrinsic, Calibrate-Once Decay Profiles. We demonstrate that the per-layer decay exponent α is an intrinsic property of the model rather than the corpus: across five models its mean lies in 0.6020.663, and single offline calibration transfers without re-tuning across WikiText, PG19, arXiv, cache budgets, and downstream QA. We further show why hiddenstate influence avoids the softmax recency bias that underestimates α in attention-mass proxies. Unified Power-Law Framework. We relate accumulation-based (H2O) and recency-based (StreamingLLM) KV-cache eviction as opposite endpoints (α = 0 and α ) of single power-law decay family si = Ai (t i)α; the family is modeling choice, not consequence of the no-hair theorem, which only constrains the exponent within family. Lightweight Drop-In Algorithm (NoHairCache). We propose training-free, plug-andplay eviction scorer that integrates per-layer calibrated exponents into standard top-k eviction pipelines with negligible overhead (a single scalar multiplication per step over H2O). Cross-Domain and Downstream Gains. Across three corpora (WikiText, PG19, arXiv) and three long-context QA benchmarks (NarrativeQA, HotpotQA, Qasper), NoHairCache improves perplexity with statistical significance (paired Wilcoxon < 0.05 on every dataset), while on QA it is never worse than StreamingLLM (e.g., retaining 98.2% of full-cache accuracy on NarrativeQA at 25% cache budget vs. 91.1% for StreamingLLM) though the gains are not statistically significant (per-cell McNemar). Echoing the no-hair principle in black hole physics, which argues that complex objects collapse down to just few fundamental parameters, our findings suggest that the seemingly complex behavior of KV cache eviction policies is largely governed by single intrinsic power-law form, whose exponent profile is fixed once per model. Rather than designing increasingly intricate heuristics, future cache compression methods can build directly on "

We make three precise, separate claims and verify each. Text:"CCFinance: Reconstructing Financial News Samples from Web Archives Abstract Proprietary news archives assign stories to firms, so reproducing financial study often requires access to both the articles and the vendors assignments. CCFinance lets researchers reconstruct firm coverage under different selection rules by recording which version of story was captured, which issuers it names and how often each appears. From 14.8 million Englishlanguage records in Fine News, derived from public Common Crawl News, we identify 5.22 million distinct, consistently dated articles with issuer links. Exact reconstruction requires the restricted snapshot and derived artifacts. We compare GLiNER and Stanza on 250 modellabeled article leads and assess issuer links and story focus against blinded model judgments. Estimated precision among 475 accepted links is 96.8%. Among held-out articles with detected company, requiring three mentions raises subject precision from 31.9% to 66.1%. Across the 20172025 panel, the same rule reduces the number of issuer-years with at least 50 qualifying stories from 13,918 to 8,641. Three reimplementations examine volatility tracking, bank networks and overnight returns. In the network study, bank pairs appearing together rose from 177 to 242 across publishers between January and April 2020, but fell from 50 to 35 within Reuters. Because much of the financial news used in empirical research comes from proprietary archives, researchers seeking to reproduce published estimate often need access both to the original stories and to the vendor annotations that associate those stories with firms. Text:"CCFinance: Reconstructing Financial News Samples from Web ArchivesAbstract Proprietary news archives assign stories to firms, so reproducing financial study often requires access to both the articles and the vendors assignments. CCFinance lets researchers reconstruct firm coverage under different selection rules by recording which version of story was captured, which issuers it names and how often each appears. From 14.8 million Englishlanguage records in Fine News, derived from public Common Crawl News, we identify 5.22 million distinct, consistently dated articles with issuer links. Exact reconstruction requires the restricted snapshot and derived artifacts. We compare GLiNER and Stanza on 250 modellabeled article leads and assess issuer links and story focus against blinded model judgments. Estimated precision among 475 accepted links is 96.8%. Among held-out articles with detected company, requiring three mentions raises subject precision from 31.9% to 66.1%. Across the 20172025 panel, the same rule reduces the number of issuer-years with at least 50 qualifying stories from 13,918 to 8,641. Three reimplementations examine volatility tracking, bank networks and overnight returns. In the network study, bank pairs appearing together rose from 177 to 242 across publishers between January and April 2020, but fell from 50 to 35 within Reuters.Because much of the financial news used in empirical research comes from proprietary archives, researchers seeking to reproduce published estimate often need access both to the original stories and to the vendor annotations that associate those stories with firms. Heston and Sinha [13, Section 2], for example, use timestamps and story IDs to match proprietary NewsScope sentiment scores and firm tags to full text from the Reuters News Archive. Even with copy of the same story, two researchers can construct different firm samples if one counts every company named in an earnings report and the other counts only the firm whose earnings it reports. Earlier collections available to researchers have often covered fewer publishers or shorter periods, while the fields needed to reconstruct sample may be distributed across products. The public SEntFiN release, for example, provides entity sentiment annotations for 10,753 headlines [30], whereas study of what investors could observe may also require the article body, its publication and capture times, and the identity of the listed issuer at that date. Researchers working outside commercial subscription have generally obtained news in one of two ways, either through bounded release from publisher or vendor, or by collecting articles directly. NIST distributes the Reuters Corpus Volume 1 and the Thomson Reuters Text Research Collection (TRC2) under research agreements. The former covers year of Englishlanguage newswire and the latter the period from January 2008 to February 2009 [23]. Collecting articles from publishers or aggregators, as Dong et al. [11] do using Nasdaqs per-symbol pages, gives researchers control over the material they retain, although the pages available at the time of collection and the terms governing their use determine how much of the sample others can re1 cover. Remy and Ding [26], for example, document the withdrawal of their original Reuters and Bloomberg downloads on copyright grounds. Because direct collection revisits live sites, researchers must respect publisher terms and crawl preferences while limiting the load they place on servers. Controls that protect site from abusive traffic can challenge research crawler even when the site admits verified search crawlers [5], whose referrals can bring publishers readers and advertising revenue [10]. shared archive allows several teams to examine the same response after single fetch. This matters for replication because two visits to the same URL can return different texts. If publisher adds second companys response to story after publication, researchers collecting on different days can disagree even on which firms the story names. When both researchers can recover the same capture, they can instead examine how their respective rules treat the same passage. Common Crawl, for example, observes publisher crawl directives and slows its requests after rate limits or server errors [8]. The scale at which such an archive can be reused is evident in language model pretraining, where filtered Common Crawl data accounted for 60% of the GPT-3 training mixture by sampling weight [4, Table 2.2]. The nonprofit Common Crawl Foundation makes both its URL index and the underlying web responses available to researchers, policymakers and the public [7, 9]. While commercial search services index pages to retrieve and rank results [12], the public archive also lets researcher inspect particular version of page after the live site has changed. In 2016 the Foundation introduced CC-News, worldwide crawl of news sites whose daily Web ARChive (WARC) files record the fetched responses and their capture times [22]. Access to those copies remains subject to the source terms and the rights in their content [6]. Before an archived response can serve as article text, researcher has to identify its body and decide which copies to retain. RefinedWeb illustrates the role of filtering and deduplication in preparing web data for language model pretraining [25]. For news, Infini-News extracts article bodies and structured metadata from CC-News and indexes them with their capture provenance [16], while Fine News applies language and quality filters to those records and removes duplicate texts [31]. These transformations allow researcher who starts with cleaned body to trace it back through the extraction to an archived response. For language model the body can already serve as training example. For financial panel further decision remains, because prices and returns are observed for particular securities on particular dates. Even after this processing, single article can name several companies, report an event that concerns only one, or express different sentiment toward each [29, 30]. Named-entity recognition locates the mentions, and issuer linking assigns them dated identities that researcher can join to financial records [28]. Article-form tags distinguish, for example, news report from press release, while event labels identify the kind of development passage describes. The quotient Hρ(K) := K/(1 + mρ) {0} remembers each element of up to relative error of size ρ. Multiplication passes to the quotient, but addition does not: the sum of two classes is the set of classes of all possible sums of representatives. This makes Hρ(K) hyperfield, and the valuation of descends to it. Text:"THE NORM AXIOM AND FACTOR HYPERFIELDS ALESSANDRO LINZI Abstract. Touchard and the author asked whether every valued hyperfield, in Krasners sense with the norm axiom, is of the form Hρ(K): valued field read up to relative error ρ. The question turns on the norm axiom. If valued hyperfield is quotient /T {0} of field, the norm axiom forces = 1 + mρ for valuation on K. This completes the proof of published proposition, and it makes the question equivalent to asking whether every Krasner valued hyperfield is such quotient. For value group we prove directly that it is. Without the norm axiom the answer is negative. Layering hyperfield over non-trivial ordered abelian group gives factor hyperfield exactly when the hyperfield itself is one, so Massouross hyperfields yield hyperfields with non-trivial valuation, satisfying all the other axioms, that are not factor hyperfields. The general question remains open. 1. Introduction 1.1. The question. Let be field with valuation and value group Γ. Fix an initial segment ρ of Γ0 and let mρ be the set of elements of whose value exceeds every element of ρ. The elements of 1 + mρ form subgroup of K. The quotient Hρ(K) := K/(1 + mρ) {0} remembers each element of up to relative error of size ρ. Multiplication passes to the quotient, but addition does not: the sum of two classes is the set of classes of all possible sums of representatives. This makes Hρ(K) hyperfield, and the valuation of descends to it. For ρ = {0} one obtains the leading-term structure RV(K) used in the model theory of valued fields; for discrete Γ and ρ = {0, . . . , 1} one obtains the hyperfields studied by Krasner [7], Tolliver [14] and Lee [10]. Quotients of field, or more generally of ring, by multiplicative subgroup are classical source of hyperstructures, going back to Krasner [8]. The Krasner hyperfield and the hyperfield of signs, which Baker and Bowler use as coefficients for matroids and oriented matroids, arise in this way [1, Remark 2.7 and Examples 2.10, 2.13], and so does the hyperring structure that Connes and Consani put on the ad`ele class space of global field [4]. Krasner isolated the properties of Hρ(K) that make it valued hyperfield. The decisive one is the norm axiom: each sum is full ultrametric ball of prescribed radius. Touchard and the author [12] studied when systems of valued hyperfields come from single valued field, and at the end of their paper they asked the converse question for single hyperfield. We quote it verbatim. Question ([12, Question 1, p. 23]). Are all valued hyperfields with value group Γ of the form Hρ(K) for some valued field and some initial segment ρ of Γ0? Here valued hyperfield means hyperfield with valuation of norm ρ in the sense of [11, Definition 4.6], recalled in Definition 2.2; by [11, Proposition 4.8(i)] this is equivalent to [12, Definition 1.8], as explained after that definition. The same circle of ideas appears in two places 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 16Y20; Secondary 12J10, 12J20, 13F30. Key words and phrases. Hyperfield, valued hyperfield, factor hyperfield, Krasner valuation, norm axiom. 1 ALESSANDRO LINZI in [11]. Proposition 4.11 there states that factor hyperfield carrying Krasner valuation is of the form Hρ(K), and Remark 4.12 records that it is not known whether every Krasner valued hyperfield is factor hyperfield. Section 7, item (2), asks the analogous question for the weaker notion of valuation of [11, Definition 3.1], which consists of the axioms (V0)(V2) below (labelled (V1)(V3) there): is it true that any hyperfield admitting non-trivial valuation is factor hyperfield? 1.2. Results. The three results below show that the question is about what the norm axiom (V3) forces. With (V3), presentation as factor hyperfield is automatically presentation as Hρ(K) (Theorem A), and in the discrete case the axioms rebuild the field without any factor presentation being given (Theorem B). Without (V3), non-trivial valuation does not force factor presentation (Theorem C). factor hyperfield is quotient KT = K/T {0} of field by an arbitrary subgroup K, with the induced hyperaddition (Section 2). Every Hρ(K) is factor hyperfield, with = 1 + mρ. The first result is that the converse holds as soon as Krasner valuation is present. Theorem (completing [11, Proposition 4.11]). Let (H, val) be valued hyperfield of norm ρ and value group Γ. Suppose that is isomorphic, as hyperfield, to factor hyperfield KT . Then carries valuation with value group Γ such that = 1 + mρ(w), and the isomorphism KT is an isomorphism of valued hyperfields Hρ(K, w) (H, val). This is the statement of [11, Proposition 4.11], whose proof in [11] shows only that consists of 1-units, that is, 1 + m{0}(w). The missing equality = 1 + mρ(w) is Krasners observation [7, p. 148] in the form of [12, Lemma 1.25]; Section 3 spells out the combination. As consequence, the question of Touchard and the author is equivalent to the question of [11, Remark 4.12]: it has positive answer if and only if every Krasner valued hyperfield is factor hyperfield (Corollary 3.2). The second result settles the question for the value group Z. Theorem B. Let (H, val) be valued hyperfield of norm ρ with value group Z. Then = Hρ(K) as valued hyperfields for discretely valued field K. This can also be derived from Tollivers comparison of Krasners discretely valued hyperfields with Delignes triples [14], together with Theorem A; see Remark 4.5. Our proof follows the same route, through the ring of [14, Lemmas 4.2 and 4.3] and Delignes observation that truncated discrete valuation rings are quotients of discrete valuation rings, but it is written directly in the axioms of [12] and is self-contained apart from theorem of Hungerford [6]. Lee [10, Theorems 4.8 and 4.10] proves much more in narrower setting: for discretely valued hyperfields of mixed characteristic (0, p) with perfect residue field and length large compared with the ramification index, he obtains equivalences of categories with truncated discrete valuation rings and with complete discrete valuation rings. Theorem needs none of these hypotheses, but it is only an existence statement. The third result answers the question of [11, 7(2)] in the negative. It uses the layering MΓ of hyperfield along an ordered abelian group, due to Bowler and Su [3, Definition 4.1]: one copy of is placed in each degree γ Γ, as residue field and value group are stacked in power series field (Section 6). Theorem C. Let be hyperfi"

Text:"UniVEP: Unifying Velocity and Endpoint Prediction for Robot Manipulation Zhengtu Liang1,* Qiwei Liang1,*, Tao Lin2 Shihang Weng3 Boyang Cai1 Xiangyu Chen1 Yikeng Chen1 Jiaming Liang1 Bohan Tan1 Yu Yan1 Renjing Xu1, 1 The Hong Kong University of Science and Technology (Guangzhou) 2 Beijing Jiaotong University * Equal contribution Project leader 3 Shenzhen University Corresponding author Flow-matching robot policies usually use one prediction target throughout action generation, even though recovering actions from noise and polishing nearly clean actions bring different challenges. We systematically compare velocity prediction (V), endpoint prediction (X), and velocity-weighted endpoint prediction (XV) across multiple robot policies, benchmarks, and inference budgets. On StarVLA and LiLa-WAM, XV works best for few-step generation, while gains more from iterative refinement. We further uncover their complementary strengths across noise levels: clean actions have concentrated covariance spectra, endpoint predictions stay more stable under high noise, and velocity predictions offer more accurate corrections near clean actions. Inspired by these findings, we introduce UniVEP, which learns both prediction targets together with consistency supervision and switches from endpoint recovery to velocity refinement as noise drops. Each step only needs one network run, and joint training also boosts single-step endpoint generation. Tests on simulated and real-world manipulation show better success rates and efficiency. On RoboTwin, UniVEP hits 61.36% success with one StarVLA run, beating the best single-target baseline at sixteen runs (59.96%). With LiLa-WAM, two runs outperform the best sixteen-step single-target baseline and cut inference latency by 77.1%. Abstract Beam is a sparse Mixture-of-Experts language model optimized for coding, reasoning, and agentic workloads, using large-scale asynchronous reinforcement learning, long-context midtraining, quantization, and speculative decoding to deliver strong reasoning and safety with efficient inference.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Beam is a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with 501.3B total parameters and 23.4B active parameters, specifically built and optimized for coding, reasoning, and agentic workloads. Beam delivers frontier level reasoning, while requiring 3-4x less inference compute than many models of its class. Pretrained on 23.8T diverse, curated, high-quality tokens, Beam features a 1M-token context window achieved through a novel two-stage midtraining phase, built on verifiable, correctness-anchored data. Post-training leveraged a large-scale, fully asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) campaign across 10.5K GB300 GPUs, generating over 100M rollouts over 4 weeks of training. We believe this is one of the largest scale RL runs conducted by any open lab to date. Across our evaluation suite, capabilities continued to improve as we increased RL compute, with no sign of a plateau. Supported by deep hardware-model co-design, FP8/NVFP4 quantization, and a 1.93B-parameter speculative decoding drafter (DFlash 2), Beam maximizes serving throughput with efficient, flexible deployment. Averaged across conversation turns and harm categories, Beam refuses or pivots on 88.9% of harmful requests while complying with 81.5% of harmless requests, achieving the highest refusal/pivot rate on harmful requests among open-weight models on the Pareto frontier.

Abstract A single diffusion transformer can unify encoding, generation, and decoding by treating them as position-aligned sequence maps distinguished by a mode token and timestep, improving image generation quality and efficiency.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Latent generative models typically rely on three separate networks: an encoder that tokenizes pixels, a generator that models the latent distribution, and a decoder that maps latents back to pixels. UNITE recently merged the first two into a single generative encoder trained in one stage, but its decoder remains a separate network. We ask whether this last network can be folded in too, so that one diffusion transformer encodes, generates, and decodes. Simply letting the shared backbone decode does not help. What makes it work is to cast all three modes as position-aligned sequence maps over one fully shared backbone, distinguished only by a mode token and the timestep. The resulting model, UNIFIED, is simple: one network trained from scratch in a single stage with only reconstruction and flow-matching losses, without adversarial losses or pretrained encoders (e.g., DINO). On ImageNet-1K 256x256 at matched parameters, UNIFIED improves gFID over UNITE at three sizes (~60M, ~115M, and ~215M) and reaches 2.08 with 215M parameters, the lowest among compared models under 250M parameters. On text-to-image generation, UNIFIED-B (218M) reaches a GenEval score of 0.87 at 512x512, exceeding single-stage pixel diffusion models with around one billion parameters. Full unification of latent diffusion is thus not merely feasible but beneficial.
[24.11.2026 23:26] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается UNIFIED — одна диффузионная трансформер-модель (diffusion transformer), которая объединяет кодирование, генерацию и декодирование изображений, тогда как в латентных генеративных моделях обычно используются три отдельные сети. Все три режима представлены как позиционно выровненные последовательные отображения над общим backbone и различаются только mode token и timestep. Модель обучается с нуля за один этап только с функциями потерь reconstruction и flow-matching, без adversarial losses и предобученных энкодеров вроде DINO. Результаты показывают улучшение gFID на ImageNet-1K по сравнению с UNITE при сопоставимых параметрах, а также высокий GenEval на text-to-image генерации.", "emoji": "🧩", "title": "Один трансформер для кодирования, генерации и декодирования"}

"Abstract A single diffusion transformer can unify encoding, generation, and decoding by treating them as position-aligned sequence maps distinguished by a mode token and timestep, improving image generation quality and efficiency.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Latent generative models typically rely on three separate networks: an encoder that tokenizes pixels, a generator that models the latent distribution, and a decoder that maps latents back to pixels. UNITE recently merged the first two into a single generative encoder trained in one stage, but its decoder remains a separate network. We ask whether this last network can be folded in too, so that one diffusion transformer encodes, generates, and decodes. Simply letting the shared backbone decode does not help. What makes it work is to cast all three modes as position-aligned sequence maps over one fully shared backbone, distinguished only by a mode token and the timestep. Full unification of latent diffusion is thus not merely feasible but beneficial."

Abstract Models can use deep latent reasoning to pursue hidden objectives while generating chain-of-thought explanations that appear task-appropriate and evade output-based monitoring.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash LLMs typically solve complex tasks with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, which is also the main tool for monitoring them for misaligned behavior. However, standard transformers can also solve tasks with complete latent reasoning (CLR), carrying out the whole task internally and outputting only the final answer. Theory bounds the depth of CLR, the number of sequential steps it can carry out, and recent work measured it to be shallow. In this work, we ask whether CLR is deep enough for a model to pursue a hidden objective that requires reasoning. Such a model would generate a CoT that appears to solve the given task, while a latent multi-step computation decides its answer based on the hidden objective. First, we show that a simple auxiliary loss allows models to solve a multi-step task latently at least 5x deeper than previously reported. Then, in two common tasks, decision-making and code generation, we train a model to pursue such a hidden objective, which decides a loan or plants a vulnerability. The model's CoT justifies the output with no trace of the hidden objective, and a strong LLM monitor does not flag it. Finally, using activation patching, we find that the latent computation is completed before the model generates its CoT, which takes no part in it. Altogether, we show that a hidden objective that requires reasoning can go unseen by a monitor that reads only the output.

Abstract LoopRes enables pretrained large language models to benefit from looped computation by letting layers attend to recent earlier loops with trainable loop-aware keys, yielding consistent gains and improved test-time scaling.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Looped Transformers iterate a shared block of layers to increase computation without adding parameters, offering stronger performance and test-time scaling. However, this power has so far required training from scratch and yielded only modest gains for LLMs. Finetuning existing models into loops is far cheaper, yet also brings only marginal gains over SFT. We propose LoopRes, an effective, scalable, and simple approach that turns loops into significant and consistent gains for pretrained LLMs. Building on Attention Residuals, LoopRes lets each looped layer attend directly to the outputs of earlier loops. Since these outputs are iterates of the same layers and become harder to tell apart, we give each a trainable loop-aware key that encodes its structural identity. Attending to all earlier loops also lets layers skip intermediate ones, so we restrict each layer to a residual window of the latest W loops, forcing every loop to build incrementally on recent ones. Across 6 pretrained LLMs from 3 model families and 9 benchmarks, LoopRes delivers significant and consistent gains: at 2x compute, it improves Qwen3-1.7B-Base by 29.1% over SFT, more than doubling the gain of the state-of-the-art baseline. The gains hold on larger models up to 30B and across all three families. More importantly, from 1.7B to 8B, LoopRes scales more steeply than SFT as compute grows, reaching a higher ceiling while needing less compute on long outputs. These results establish LoopRes as an effective looping approach, delivering both better performance and better test-time scaling.

Каждый зацикленный слой обращается напрямую к выходам предыдущих итераций через обучаемые loop-aware ключи, а внимание ограничено скользящим окном из последних W циклов. Abstract WorldLatent unifies 4D perception and generation in a shared multimodal latent space, enabling a single model to perform zero-shot 4D perception, coherent 4D generation, and controllable editing.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Generative Pre-Training (GPT) represents a profound milestone in natural language processing, serving as the foundation for most of today's state-of-the-art large language models. By casting diverse understanding and generation problems into a shared token representation, GPT enables a single architecture and unified optimization loop to master heterogeneous tasks across massive corpora, giving rise to powerful foundation models for natural language. In contrast, 4D spatiotemporal vision lacks a common representational protocol: perception (such as depth and camera estimation) and generation are either handled by separate one-way pipelines or bolted together from unaligned single-modality spaces. In this work, we introduce WorldLatent, a framework that formulates a unified 4D latent space as a common protocol for both world perception and generation. Rather than relying on fragmented, task-specific models or stacked single-modality representations, our framework proposes a multimodal VAE architecture that compresses and fuses complex 4D visual appearance, geometry, and dynamics into a single structured latent representation. Within this formulation, perception and generation operate as complementary denoising processes over the same latent space, enabling one generative model to learn both capabilities simultaneously within a single training loop. Experiments across representative 4D benchmarks verify that our fused latent protocol achieves specialist-level zero-shot 4D perception, coherent 4D generation, and controllable editing from a single model. By demonstrating that diverse 4D tasks can be seamlessly unified under a shared representational protocol, our work provides a scalable foundation for general-purpose 4D spatial intelligence.

Используется архитектура multimodal VAE, которая сжимает и объединяет внешний вид, геометрию и динамику сцены в одно структурированное латентное представление. Abstract Adopting reasoning-oriented post-training recipes for knowledge tasks leads models to memorize self-generated hallucinations, especially on difficult questions, and makes them less likely to abstain from those false facts later.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Reasoning domains have shaped the post-training recipes of frontier models even though they are trained and deployed in domains beyond math and code. In this paper, we study the consequences of directly adopting reasoning-centric training recipes, e.g., the use of outcome-based rewards for reinforcement learning (RL), injection of "cognitive" behaviors such as proposing and backtracking from hypotheses in reasoning chains, etc., for knowledge tasks. We hypothesize that these decisions, while useful for reasoning, inadvertently encourage models to memorize hallucinations they themselves generate in their long reasoning chains when trained on knowledge tasks. To study this, we train models with standard post-training, extract their self-generated hallucinations during RL, and track memorization throughout the rest of training. Our experiments on six open models across two model families and two architectures confirm our hypothesis. We find that models' recall of hallucinations reinforced during training increases by 4.83% on average, compared to just 0.17% for self-hallucinations that receive no training signal. In fact, models even memorize false facts that they immediately retract in their reasoning; a backtracking template inherited from reasoning-centric training that has no benefit for knowledge recall. Furthermore, we find that memorization is strongest on the hardest training questions, and rewarding a hallucination makes the model less likely to abstain from it later (by 7.23% on average). Overall, our work identifies and systematically studies a previously unexamined pathway through which models learn hallucinations during training.

Авторы извлекают самогенерированные ошибки во время RL и отслеживают их запоминание, показывая рост recall поощряемых галлюцинаций на 4.83% против 0.17% для ненагружаемых. Эффект наиболее силён на самых сложных вопросах, а поощрение галлюцинации снижает вероятность последующего abstain на 7.23%. Abstract GlanceNav is a streaming multimodal navigator that maintains constant per-step memory through recurrent and attention mechanisms and uses a reinforcement learning correction method to improve vision-and-language navigation.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong policies for vision-and-language navigation (VLN). However, most of them encode a sample of past frames again at every decision and are trained on isolated decisions, so computation is redundant and the usable history is short. They also learn from their own mistakes through outcome rewards that give one scalar to a whole episode. We ask whether a navigator can encode each frame only once, at a constant cost per step, and still remember what matters and learn efficiently from its mistakes. We present GlanceNav, a streaming navigator built on a hybrid MLLM. Its Gated DeltaNet layers carry a recurrent state through the whole episode, while its attention layers keep a short window of recent observations and a small bank of keyframes, selected by how much each frame changes the recurrent state. This constant-size memory keeps the cost per step fixed and allows training on whole episodes with the same memory as at inference. Because the entire memory can also be copied in constant time, we further propose Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), a reinforcement learning stage that rewinds a drifting rollout to its closest approach to the goal, lets an expert correct a few actions, and credits decisions by the resulting change in outcome. Trained only on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE training splits, with 1,000 instructions for reinforcement learning, a 4B GlanceNav reaches success rates of 71.9% and 73.6% on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen. On R2R-CE, it outperforms the strongest previous single-RGB method trained without external data by 9.1 points, while running at 104 ms per step.
[24.11.2026 23:26] Response: {"desc": "GlanceNav — потоковый навигатор на основе гибридной MLLM, который кодирует каждый кадр только один раз и поддерживает постоянный объём памяти на каждом шаге. Рекуррентные слои Gated DeltaNet передают состояние через весь эпизод, а слои внимания хранят короткое окно последних наблюдений и небольшой набор ключевых кадров. Для обучения с подкреплением авторы предлагают метод Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), который откатывает траекторию к точке наибольшего приближения к цели, позволяет эксперту исправить несколько действий и оценивает решения по изменению итогового результата. На R2R-CE и RxR-CE модель достигает 71,9% и 73,6% успешных навигаций, а на R2R-CE опережает лучший предыдущий метод с одним RGB-входом на 9,1 пункта при 104 мс на шаг.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Один взгляд на кадр — постоянная память и умное исправление ошибок в навигации"}

Abstract  Language model agents are increasingly asked to run ongoing multi-day operations rather than finish isolated single-turn tasks. In daily operations, today's choices reshape later observations and cash constraints because empty shelves hide lost walk-in demand, short-shipped customers quietly shrink future orders weeks later, and emergency buying drains working capital. We introduce SMB Bench, a calibrated 90-day wholesale simulator with 18 causally paired tasks and an 859-run frontier trace dataset, to ask what experience and learning signal make an agent better at noticing, attributing, and acting on the delayed consequences of its own decisions in unseen businesses. Across 16 frontier models, only 3 outperform a fixed buying rule; models often record a relevant diagnosis in their notes without changing later purchases in time, showing that diagnosing a problem, changing behavior, and improving a business are separate achievements. To study how experience improves those failures in an open model (Qwen3.8-27B), we used training-business records to identify operational rules and archived frontier quarters as starting states to continue from rather than transcripts to copy. On an unseen test business under identical evaluation instructions, the trained policy followed customer-protection commitments more consistently and reduced severe-loss quarters, while the additional profit benefit remained uncertain. Carrying those commitments through the quarter exposed the cost of fulfilling them, as early-quarter stocking habits left the buyer relying on emergency supply and a deteriorating supplier. The results separate recognizing a delayed effect, acting on it consistently, and coordinating surrounding decisions so that the response improves profit. SMB Bench connects these observations to a learning method by turning archived decisions and their consequences into reusable training experience.

Из 16 моделей лишь 3 превзошли фиксированное правило закупок, и часто агент правильно диагностировал проблему в заметках, но не менял закупки вовремя. To study how experience improves those failures in an open model (Qwen3.8-27B), we used training-business records to identify operational rules and archived frontier quarters as starting states to continue from rather than transcripts to copy. On an unseen test business under identical evaluation instructions, the trained policy followed customer-protection commitments more consistently and reduced severe-loss quarters, while the additional profit benefit remained uncertain. Carrying those commitments through the quarter exposed the cost of fulfilling them, as early-quarter stocking habits left the buyer relying on emergency supply and a deteriorating supplier. The results separate recognizing a delayed effect, acting on it consistently, and coordinating surrounding decisions so that the response improves profit. SMB Bench connects these observations to a learning method by turning archived decisions and their consequences into reusable training experience."

Abstract A benchmarking pipeline evaluates coding agents on optimizing CPU kernels across evolving Arm architectural extensions, showing strong gains on established SIMD extensions but unreliable adaptation to newer matrix-tile programming models.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Efficient AI computation depends on hardware that evolves over time, shifting the strategies needed to implement performant code. For CPU kernels, a key evolving factor is the set of available architectural extensions, which expose specialized hardware instructions that change the techniques used to optimize kernels. We study how effectively coding agents use these new, potentially unfamiliar extensions to write fast code. We introduce KernelShift, a benchmarking pipeline and a set of environments for evaluating coding agents in kernel optimization across evolving CPU architectures. Its agent-assisted, human-verified pipeline turns code repositories into benchmark instances, and its environments let agents optimize code with feedback from the target hardware. Using this pipeline, we construct 99 kernels from real-world, actively developed repositories (ncnn, llama.cpp, Arm SIMD Loops, and KleidiAI), including inference workloads derived from real model executions. KernelShift supports incorporating new kernels and hardware configurations as libraries and processors evolve. We evaluate agents with proprietary and open-weight models across progressively newer architectural extensions (Arm Neon, SVE, SVE2, and SME2), examining how performance changes as hardware evolves. On established extensions, agents beat the auto-vectorized compiler everywhere (2.4-19.8x) and exceed general-purpose libraries such as ncnn (getting up to 2.74x over its Neon kernels), but fall behind Arm's hand-tuned KleidiAI kernels (0.70x). In end-to-end inference of two LLMs, agent-written kernels achieve a 1.3-1.6x prefill speedup over llama.cpp's fast path. However, current agents do not yet reliably adapt to new extensions that require unfamiliar programming models, such as SME2's matrix tiles.

Агенты с обратной связью от реального железа на 99 ядрах из репозиториев ncnn, llama.cpp и KleidiAI превосходят авто-векторизацию компилятора (2.4–19.8x) и ncnn, но уступают вручную оптимизированным ядрам KleidiAI (0.70x). Abstract Innata-0 is a native embodied world system that co-designs a world-action model and an agent to enable reliable long-horizon physical behaviors through integrated understanding, prediction, action generation, memory, verification, and recovery.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash The physical world is open-ended, dynamic, and partially observable. While foundation models have advanced embodied intelligence, standalone models struggle with long-horizon execution, and simply integrating models with agents may lead to inconsistencies between planning and physical control. To support reliable and adaptive behaviors in the physical world, we introduce Innata-0, a native embodied world system. Innata-0 jointly designs an embodied world-action model and an agent to combine their complementary strengths. The model, Innata-0-WAM, adopts a tri-tower Mixture-of-Transformers architecture that integrates understanding, prediction, and action generation. The agent, Innata-0-Agent, coordinates long-horizon execution through memory, verification, and recovery. A co-design protocol is further proposed to align model training with agent execution and leverage validated experiences to support subsequent adaptation and model improvement. Extensive evaluations demonstrate strong embodied capabilities across understanding, world prediction, and robotic manipulation. Innata-0 represents our first step toward physical intelligence, and we see it as the beginning of a much longer journey.

Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract  Answering questions over days or weeks of video requires searching for scattered clues across long timelines. Existing video agents rely on complex memory graphs and fixed retrieval pipelines, which restrict how the agent can search. We propose VideoJSON, a minimal harness for ultra-long video understanding that keeps the memory simple and lets a coding agent write its own search programs. During indexing, a vision-language model turns each minute of video into a single JSON record containing a dense caption and structured fields such as events, entities, places, and speech transcripts. At query time, the agent writes Python or Bash programs in a sandboxed container to filter, link, and read these records over multiple steps, without task-specific training or raw video access. On four ultra-long video benchmarks, VideoJSON with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash outperforms MAGIC-Video by 7.0, 20.0, and 24.75 points on EgoLifeQA, Ego-R1-Bench, and MM-Lifelong Week, and beats the best prior EgoMemReason score by 12.6 points. Across four policy models of increasing capability, VideoJSON's average score rises by up to 11.58 points over the weakest model, compared with at most 5.50 points for MAGIC-Video. Ablations show that both agentic code search and the structured JSON records drive these gains, while adding hourly summaries, extra tools, or video access changes the average score by less than 1.5 points. In addition, fine-tuning Qwen-3.5-Flash on 64 VideoJSON trajectories from an unrelated 100-hour livestream improves its MM-Lifelong scores by up to 8.25 points. Code will be open-sourced after publication.
[24.11.2026 23:27] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет VideoJSON — минимальный фреймворк для понимания сверхдлинных видео (дни и недели записей), где на этапе индексирования vision-language модель превращает каждую минуту видео в JSON-запись с плотным описанием и структурированными полями. На этапе запроса LLM-агент сам пишет программы на Python или Bash в изолированном контейнере, фильтруя и связывая записи без дообучения под конкретную задачу. На четырёх бенчмарках VideoJSON превосходит MAGIC-Video и лучшие предыдущие методы, а ablation-исследования показывают, что ключевой вклад дают агентный поиск кодом и структурированные JSON-записи. Abstract Mutual Information Prototype Alignment distills datasets for self-supervised learning by optimizing synthetic prototypes to preserve augmentation-invariant information and improve learned representations.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Dataset distillation (DD) aims to compress a large dataset into a small synthetic one that remains effective for training neural networks. Despite its success in supervised learning, DD remains underexplored in self-supervised learning (SSL), where distilled data must preserve the information needed to learn representations that remain consistent across augmentations. To bridge this gap, we introduce Mutual Information Prototype Alignment (MIPA), an efficient SSL dataset distillation approach that optimizes synthetic images to preserve augmentation-invariant information. Specifically, MIPA treats synthetic images as learnable prototypes and uses a frozen encoder to assign real samples to them based on feature similarity. A synthetic dataset that preserves such augmentation invariance should yield stable prototype assignments across multiple augmented views of the same real image. MIPA encourages this by maximizing mutual information between cross-view assignments while avoiding trivial solutions in which all real samples are assigned to only a few synthetic prototypes. Evaluations on image classification benchmarks show that synthetic data generated by MIPA yield higher-quality representations than existing distillation approaches while reducing time and storage demands during distillation.
[24.11.2026 23:27] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается метод Mutual Information Prototype Alignment (MIPA) для дистилляции датасетов в задачах self-supervised learning. Метод максимизирует взаимную информацию между назначениями прототипов для разных аугментаций одного изображения, избегая тривиальных решений с перекосом в назначениях. Abstract ApexID, a hierarchical SphereFace2-based loss combining global identity discrimination with balanced category supervision, improves animal re-identification and transfer to unseen datasets and species, though performance varies by backbone and open-set evaluation remains challenging.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Animal re-identification (Re-ID) must distinguish individuals across species and capture conditions. We introduce ApexID, a hierarchical SphereFace2-based loss that integrates global identity discrimination with balanced animal-category supervision. Using 132,533 images curated from 19 public datasets, we evaluate three model families and four objectives on four external datasets with observation-aware retrieval protocols. ApexID achieves a dataset-averaged Rank-1 accuracy of 64.89% with ConvNeXt-Small and an mAP of 32.54% with MIEW-ID, outperforming their pretrained reference models. On elephants, a category absent from animal Re-ID training, our ConvNeXt-Small variant achieves the highest retrieval performance among the fourteen evaluated configurations. Abstract PixelOne transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student to predict GUI clicks at native resolution in one forward pass, achieving high accuracy and low latency.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash GUI grounding requires precise clicks at low latency. Recent models such as GUI-Decisions and GUI-Swift predict coordinates in one forward pass using fixed global bins with local refinement. However, screenshot downsampling removes detail needed to locate small controls, while fixed bins cover larger pixel ranges at higher resolutions. We introduce PixelOne, which transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student that predicts clicks directly from screenshots at native resolution. The teacher uses VideoJEV's text-option scorer to select rows and columns and progressively zoom into candidate regions. The student inherits the teacher's parameters and matches its distributions over explored regions through spatial distillation alongside box supervision. At inference, the same decision head selects a visual token, and a lightweight pixel head predicts the click within it, all in one forward pass. A click-aligned loss encourages pixels inside the target box to score above those outside. Across four benchmarks, PixelOne-Pro-35B-A3B achieves 83.8% mean accuracy, the highest among the evaluated single-forward grounders, with a mean latency of 0.43 s per target.
[24.11.2026 23:27] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен PixelOne — метод для предсказания кликов по элементам GUI за один проход модели. Знания о пространственной структуре передаются от «учителя» с поиском по регионам к «ученику», который работает напрямую с нативным разрешением скриншота, без потери мелких деталей. Ученик перенимает параметры учителя и обучается через spatial distillation вместе с box supervision, а лёгкая pixel head уточняет точку клика внутри выбранного visual token. Благодаря click-aligned loss модель достигает 83.8% средней точности на четырёх бенчмарках при задержке около 0.43 с.", "emoji": "🎯", "title": "Точный клик за один проход: пиксель в пиксель"}

"Abstract PixelOne transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student to predict GUI clicks at native resolution in one forward pass, achieving high accuracy and low latency.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash GUI grounding requires precise clicks at low latency. Recent models such as GUI-Decisions and GUI-Swift predict coordinates in one forward pass using fixed global bins with local refinement. Abstract Frontier models can identify whether text was written by an AI or human, often which lab or exact author, threatening anonymity for prolific online writers.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Given a single text and a simple zero-shot prompt (no web access, tools, candidate lists, or writing samples), frontier models can answer "who wrote this?" at three increasingly specific levels: (1) whether an AI or human wrote it, (2) if an AI, which lab, and (3) if a human, often exactly who. The latest models (Claude Fable 5.2, GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 4 Carbon) match trained detectors like Pangram while also explaining their predictions in natural language, and Opus 5.5 names the correct lab for 98% of texts generated by models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google. Accuracy depends on exposure to an author's writing in pretraining data and on reasoning effort, and simply asking a model how familiar it is with someone predicts whether it can identify their writing. For anyone who has published enough writing online, frontier models present a greater threat to anonymity than commonly assumed, and defenses are limited.

Abstract  Agentic systems are increasingly being explored for automating experimental high-energy physics measurements, but the correctness of their results can today be reliably evaluated only via extensive expert review. To address this, we propose a method to perform detailed quantitative evaluations of agent-driven analyses through the use of blind analysis and injection tests, which are critical in a typical expert review. Agents develop a data analysis pipeline on a public dataset, while the evaluation is performed on programmatically generated sealed private datasets where signals are injected and simulation settings varied within realistic detector uncertainties. By requiring agents to provide a single workflow that is evaluated across these "sealed worlds", we are able to test the accuracy and robustness of the workflows. We demonstrate the approach on four tasks built from CMS open data and present it as a way to reliably benchmark scientific work in experimental high-energy physics. At the time of writing, the best-performing model, Gemini 4 Argon, reaches 60% of the score of the expert reference analyses on average, ahead of GPT 6 Astra at 38% and Claude Opus 5.5 at 31%. In individual sessions on one of the tasks, Gemini 4 Argon exceeds the reference. The benchmark is hosted at https://hep-ex-bench.github.io

Abstract Lyris uses persistent point-cloud memory and vision-language-model-authored 3D layouts to generate long, camera-controlled walkthroughs from a single photo, improving camera accuracy and return consistency.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Exploring a scene from as little as a single photo requires generating a long, camera-controlled walkthrough that extends far beyond what was captured, yet remains consistent upon return. Memory-based video generation methods preserve what they have already seen, but have no anchor beyond it, where the camera drifts from its path and a text prompt can name objects but not place them. Layout-conditioned methods provide such an anchor and precise control over what appears where, but rely on layouts that are typically authored by hand. Both limitations arise because, beyond what was captured, nothing anchors the video model unless a human authors one. To overcome both, we propose Lyris, a long video generation framework that never leaves the video model without an anchor: a persistent point-cloud memory where the scene has been observed or generated, and a 3D layout that a vision-language model authors where it has not. Our key insight is that the interface between the two models can be surprisingly coarse. A layout of one labeled 3D box per object is simple enough for a vision-language model to author (and for a user to edit), yet expressive enough for a video model to follow once rendered along the camera path. We demonstrate walkthroughs of over three minutes and 50 meters from a single photo, passing through doorways into rooms it never shows. When evaluated on minute-long round trips across four datasets, Lyris outperforms state-of-the-art methods by a wide margin, reducing camera error by 3.8x and improving consistency upon return by 22% on average.

Для памяти сцены используется постоянное облако точек (point-cloud memory), а для областей, которые не были показаны, vision-language model (VLM) создаёт 3D-раскладку из размеченных боксов объектов. Abstract CCFinance reconstructs firm coverage from public news archives under different selection rules, using model-assisted labeling and validation, and shows how mention thresholds affect issuer-year counts and financial network measures.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Proprietary news archives assign stories to firms, so reproducing a financial study often requires access to both the articles and the vendor's assignments. CCFinance lets researchers reconstruct firm coverage under different selection rules by recording which version of a story was captured, which issuers it names and how often each appears. From 14.8 million English-language records in Fine News, derived from public Common Crawl News, we identify 5.22 million distinct, consistently dated articles with issuer links. Exact reconstruction requires the restricted snapshot and derived artifacts. We compare GLiNER and Stanza on 250 model-labeled article leads and assess issuer links and story focus against blinded model judgments. Estimated precision among 475 accepted links is 96.8%. Among held-out articles with a detected company, requiring three mentions raises subject precision from 31.9% to 66.1%. Across the 2017-2025 panel, the same rule reduces the number of issuer-years with at least 50 qualifying stories from 13,918 to 8,641. Three reimplementations examine volatility tracking, bank networks and overnight returns. In the network study, bank pairs appearing together rose from 177 to 242 across publishers between January and April 2020, but fell from 50 to 35 within Reuters.
